### Online Anti-sexist Speech: Identifying Resistance to Gender Bias in Political Discourse
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11434v1)
- **Authors**: Aditi Dutta, Susan Banducci
- **Abstract**: Anti-sexist speech, i.e., public expressions that challenge or resist gendered abuse and sexism, plays a vital role in shaping democratic debate online. Yet automated content moderation systems, increasingly powered by large language models (LLMs), may struggle to distinguish such resistance from the sexism it opposes. This study examines how five LLMs classify sexist, anti-sexist, and neutral political tweets from the UK, focusing on high-salience trigger events involving female Members of Parliament in the year 2022. Our analysis show that models frequently misclassify anti-sexist speech as harmful, particularly during politically charged events where rhetorical styles of harm and resistance converge. These errors risk silencing those who challenge sexism, with disproportionate consequences for marginalised voices. We argue that moderation design must move beyond binary harmful/not-harmful schemas, integrate human-in-the-loop review during sensitive events, and explicitly include counter-speech in training data. By linking feminist scholarship, event-based analysis, and model evaluation, this work highlights the sociotechnical challenges of safeguarding resistance speech in digital political spaces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the challenges of detecting anti-sexist speech online using large language models (LLMs). It argues that current content moderation systems, often powered by LLMs, struggle to distinguish between anti-sexist speech (which challenges sexism) and the sexism it opposes. This misclassification can lead to the silencing of marginalized voices and a reinforcement of existing power dynamics. The study evaluates the performance of several LLMs in classifying sexist, anti-sexist, and neutral political tweets from the UK, focusing on trigger events involving female Members of Parliament. The analysis demonstrates that models frequently misclassify anti-sexist speech as harmful, particularly during politically charged events where the rhetorical styles of harm and resistance converge. The authors advocate for a shift in moderation design that moves beyond binary harmful/not-harmful classifications, integrates human review during sensitive events, and explicitly includes counter-speech in training data. They use feminist scholarship, event-based analysis, and model evaluation to highlight the sociotechnical challenges of safeguarding resistance speech in digital political spaces. The paper also examines model confidence and uncertainty through metrics like entropy and perplexity, finding that high confidence does not always correlate with accuracy, especially when classifying anti-sexist speech.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its focus on *anti-sexist* speech as a distinct category worthy of dedicated analysis within the context of automated content moderation. While prior work has addressed sexism detection and mitigation, examining the specific challenges and biases related to identifying and preserving *resistance* speech is a valuable contribution. The combination of feminist theory, event-based analysis in a UK political context, and in-depth model evaluation further strengthens the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it raises crucial ethical and practical concerns about the potential for automated systems to inadvertently suppress marginalized voices and reinforce existing inequalities. The findings demonstrate that current approaches to content moderation may not adequately protect counter-speech, which is essential for fostering healthy democratic discourse online. By highlighting the limitations of LLMs in distinguishing between harmful and resistant language, the study provides valuable insights for developing more equitable and effective content moderation strategies. This has direct relevance to social media platforms, policymakers, and researchers working on AI ethics and fairness.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong theoretical grounding:** The paper effectively integrates feminist theory and social psychology to frame its analysis of anti-sexism and its importance in challenging patriarchal norms.
    *   **Rigorous methodology:** The use of event-based analysis, prompt engineering, and uncertainty metrics provides a comprehensive assessment of LLM performance and limitations.
    *   **Real-world relevance:** The focus on UK political discourse and trigger events grounds the analysis in a specific and timely context.
    *   **Clear policy recommendations:** The paper offers concrete recommendations for improving moderation practices, such as moving beyond binary classifications and incorporating human review.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scope of datasets:** While the study focuses on a specific context (UK political tweets), the findings may not be generalizable to other languages, cultures, or platforms. More diverse and larger datasets could strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Model limitations:** The LLMs used are relatively "smaller" models, which may limit the findings compared to very large, closed-source models (e.g., GPT-4).
    *   **Simplified prompt engineering:** The types of prompts, though varying in instruction style, are relatively straightforward. A more comprehensive exploration of prompt engineering techniques may further improve model performance.
    *  **Lack of a comparative baseline:** There is no baseline measurement. Adding a measurement from classical Machine Learning or lexicon-based models would provide a good comparison measurement.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of content moderation systems, particularly on social media platforms. It could also spur further research into the development of more nuanced and context-aware AI models that are better able to identify and protect counter-speech. The paper's interdisciplinary approach and clear policy recommendations make it accessible and relevant to a wide audience, including researchers, policymakers, and platform developers.

**Overall Assessment and Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and timely contribution to the field of AI ethics and content moderation. While there are some limitations in terms of dataset diversity and prompt design, the study's novel focus on anti-sexist speech, its rigorous methodology, and its clear policy recommendations make it a significant work. The paper raises crucial concerns about the potential for automated systems to inadvertently silence marginalized voices and reinforce existing inequalities, and it offers concrete steps for mitigating these risks.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale for the Score:** The paper demonstrates solid novelty and significant impact, but could be strengthened through additional datasets, analysis of diverse prompting styles, addition of comparison to baseline models, and exploration of larger LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Inclusion Arena: An Open Platform for Evaluating Large Foundation Models with Real-World Apps
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11452v1)
- **Authors**: Kangyu Wang, Hongliang He, Lin Liu, Ruiqi Liang, Zhenzhong Lan, Jianguo Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have ushered in a new era of AI capabilities, demonstrating near-human-level performance across diverse scenarios. While numerous benchmarks (e.g., MMLU) and leaderboards (e.g., Chatbot Arena) have been proposed to help evolve the development of LLMs and MLLMs, most rely on static datasets or crowdsourced general-domain prompts, often falling short of reflecting performance in real-world applications. To bridge this critical gap, we present Inclusion Arena, a live leaderboard that ranks models based on human feedback collected directly from AI-powered applications. Our platform integrates pairwise model comparisons into natural user interactions, ensuring evaluations reflect practical usage scenarios. For robust model ranking, we employ the Bradley-Terry model augmented with two key innovations: (1) Placement Matches, a cold-start mechanism to quickly estimate initial ratings for newly integrated models, and (2) Proximity Sampling, an intelligent comparison strategy that prioritizes battles between models of similar capabilities to maximize information gain and enhance rating stability. Extensive empirical analyses and simulations demonstrate that Inclusion Arena yields reliable and stable rankings, exhibits higher data transitivity compared to general crowdsourced datasets, and significantly mitigates the risk of malicious manipulation. By fostering an open alliance between foundation models and real-world applications, Inclusion Arena aims to accelerate the development of LLMs and MLLMs truly optimized for practical, user-centric deployments. The platform is publicly accessible at https://doraemon.alipay.com/model-ranking.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Inclusion Arena, a novel platform for evaluating large language models (LLMs) and multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) based on human feedback collected directly from real-world AI-powered applications. The platform integrates pairwise model comparisons into natural user interactions and employs a Bradley-Terry model for ranking, augmented with Placement Matches (a cold-start mechanism for new models) and Proximity Sampling (a strategy to compare models of similar capabilities for maximal information gain). The authors present the system architecture, engineering details, and conduct empirical analyses and simulations demonstrating the reliability, stability, and data transitivity of Inclusion Arena's rankings, as well as its resilience against manipulation. The paper aims to foster an open alliance between foundation models and real-world applications, thereby accelerating the development of LLMs and MLLMs optimized for practical, user-centric deployments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a notable degree of novelty, particularly in its *integration of model evaluation within real-world AI applications*.  While existing platforms like Chatbot Arena also use pairwise comparisons and Elo-like ranking, Inclusion Arena distinguishes itself by drawing data directly from actual app usage, rather than relying on crowdsourced or static benchmarks. The *Proximity Sampling* strategy also represents a novel contribution to efficient model comparison, explicitly targeting matchups that provide maximal information gain. The cold-start mechanism of *Placement Matches* is a practical addition that facilitates quicker integration of new models.

**Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to bridge the gap between academic benchmarks and real-world performance. LLMs and MLLMs often perform differently in controlled evaluations versus practical deployments, making insights derived from real user interactions highly valuable. Inclusion Arena can provide a more accurate and nuanced understanding of model strengths and weaknesses in authentic usage scenarios. The focus on data from "AI-powered applications" ensures that the evaluations are geared towards models that can solve real problems that are deemed useful by the end-users of those apps. The *scalability* of the system and its built-in protection against data manipulation are additional strengths that could significantly impact how models are developed and deployed.
However, it is important to critically assess the following:

*   **Application Dependence:** The current data is limited to two applications (Joyland and T-Box), which could introduce bias depending on their specific user bases and task domains. The generalizability of the platform's findings requires further validation across a wider range of applications. The "real-world data" being utilized is tied to the applications being evaluated, which may not always reflect true "real-world" scenarios.
*   **Multimodal Limitations:** While the platform aims to evaluate MLLMs, current support is primarily focused on language-based interactions. Extending the framework to fully leverage multimodal capabilities (e.g., image understanding, video processing) would increase its relevance.
*   **Transparency of Data and Models:** While the authors plan to open-source some data, greater transparency regarding the types of applications and specific model configurations could further enhance reproducibility and trust in the platform's results.
*   **Algorithmic Bias:** The framework may be susceptible to feedback loops, where models prioritized by the algorithm receive more data, thus further increasing their rankings.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. This score acknowledges the paper's strengths in novel data collection methodology and the innovative ranking strategy that improves evaluation efficiency by focusing on more informative pairwise battles, but also accounts for the limitations in scope and generalizability. The use of a real-world evaluation with protections against data manipulation offers important insight into model comparison. Future work to incorporate more applications, multimodal support, and wider domain coverage would significantly increase its overall impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reference Points in LLM Sentiment Analysis: The Role of Structured Context
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11454v1)
- **Authors**: Junichiro Niimi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are now widely used across many fields, including marketing research. Sentiment analysis, in particular, helps firms understand consumer preferences. While most NLP studies classify sentiment from review text alone, marketing theories, such as prospect theory and expectation--disconfirmation theory, point out that customer evaluations are shaped not only by the actual experience but also by additional reference points. This study therefore investigates how the content and format of such supplementary information affect sentiment analysis using LLMs. We compare natural language (NL) and JSON-formatted prompts using a lightweight 3B parameter model suitable for practical marketing applications. Experiments on two Yelp categories (Restaurant and Nightlife) show that the JSON prompt with additional information outperforms all baselines without fine-tuning: Macro-F1 rises by 1.6% and 4% while RMSE falls by 16% and 9.1%, respectively, making it deployable in resource-constrained edge devices. Furthermore, a follow-up analysis confirms that performance gains stem from genuine contextual reasoning rather than label proxying. This work demonstrates that structured prompting can enable smaller models to achieve competitive performance, offering a practical alternative to large-scale model deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores how incorporating structured context as "reference points" from marketing theories (prospect theory and expectation-disconfirmation theory) affects the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in sentiment analysis.  The study compares natural language (NL) and JSON-formatted prompts, using a lightweight 3B parameter model on Yelp reviews.  The key findings are that JSON-formatted prompts with additional contextual information (user average rating, business average rating, etc.) significantly outperform baselines, even without fine-tuning.  The paper further analyzes whether these performance gains are due to genuine contextual reasoning or simply proxying for the labels, and how interactions between multiple reference points affect prediction accuracy.  The authors argue that structured prompting allows smaller models to achieve competitive performance, offering a practical alternative to large-scale model deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating contextual information from marketing theories into LLM sentiment analysis is interesting and adds a layer of nuance beyond traditional text-based sentiment analysis. The comparison between NL and JSON prompts is a well-structured evaluation point.  The investigation into whether reference points act as label proxies is a good sanity check. However, the use of Yelp dataset and LLama-3.2-3B, are incremental contributions since these resources are heavily adopted in marketing/nlp field.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in demonstrating a practical approach for improving the accuracy of smaller LLMs in sentiment analysis through structured prompting. This is particularly relevant for real-world applications where computational resources are limited. The findings suggest that businesses can leverage their existing data (user histories, business metrics) to enhance sentiment analysis without relying on massive, computationally expensive models.  The adaptive inference strategy is a valuable contribution.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper is well-structured and clearly articulates the research questions, methodology, and findings.
    * The experimental design is rigorous, with a controlled comparison between different prompting strategies and baselines.
    * The follow-up analysis to rule out label proxying strengthens the validity of the results.
    * The results are practically relevant, demonstrating how to improve the performance of LLMs in a real-world marketing context.
    * Well-defined contribution to improving the practical deployment of sentiment analysis system and identifying the limitations on current large language model.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The scope of the study is limited to English-language Yelp reviews and a single LLM architecture (Llama-3.2-3B).  Generalizability to other languages, domains, and models needs to be confirmed.
    * While the paper argues for computational efficiency, it lacks quantitative measurements of inference time and memory usage across different prompt formats.
    * The reliance on prospect theory and EDT, while theoretically motivated, doesn't include direct empirical tests of the underlying psychological mechanisms.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a practical and effective way to improve LLM sentiment analysis using structured contextual information. However, the limitations regarding generalizability, lack of quantitative performance metrics, and the absence of direct tests of the psychological theories prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper presents an interesting insight and clear experiments. The novelty is sufficient, and the potential impact on real-world applications is clear.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TRACY: Benchmarking Execution Efficiency of LLM-Based Code Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11468v1)
- **Authors**: Zhihao Gong, Zeyu Sun, Dong Huang, Qingyuan Liang, Jie M. Zhang, Dan Hao
- **Abstract**: Automatic code translation is a fundamental task in modern software development. While the advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) has significantly improved the correctness of code translation, the critical dimension of execution efficiency remains overlooked. To address this gap, we introduce TRACY, the first comprehensive benchmark designed to evaluate the execution efficiency of LLM-translated code. TRACY is constructed through an LLM-driven two-stage pipeline: an initial stage generates a suite of stress tests to amplify performance differences, followed by an efficiency-oriented task pruning stage that isolates the efficiency-distinguishing tasks. The resulting benchmark comprises 1,011 code translation tasks across C++, Java, and Python, each accompanied by an average of 22.1 verified reference translations and 10 computationally demanding tests. Our extensive evaluation of 26 representative LLMs reveals that even top-tier LLMs struggle to consistently produce efficient code translations. For instance, Claude-4-think, the leading model for correctness, ranks eighth overall when time efficiency is taken into account, surpassed by several smaller open-source models. We further pinpoint that algorithmic flaws and improper resource handling are the most detrimental, causing a median time slowdown of 5.6$\times$ and memory increase of 12.0$\times$, respectively. Our work underscores the necessity of jointly optimizing for correctness and efficiency in future LLM-based code translation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRACY, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the execution efficiency of code translations generated by Large Language Models (LLMs).  Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily focus on correctness, TRACY emphasizes performance aspects such as execution time and memory usage.  The benchmark construction involves a two-stage process: (1) an LLM-driven stress test generation phase to amplify performance differences and (2) an efficiency-oriented task pruning phase to isolate tasks where efficiency matters. The authors evaluated 26 LLMs using TRACY, revealing a disconnect between functional correctness and efficiency. The analysis highlights common inefficiency patterns such as algorithmic flaws, suboptimal idiom usage, and improper resource handling, quantified in terms of time slowdown and memory increase.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a significant and overlooked gap in the current LLM evaluation landscape. While previous work has focused on the functional correctness of code generation, TRACY is the first comprehensive benchmark specifically designed to assess execution efficiency. This shift in focus is important because functionally correct but inefficient code can severely impact real-world applications. The methodology for stress test generation using LLMs is interesting and scalable. The detailed root cause analysis and taxonomy of inefficiencies adds further value.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are highly relevant for the LLM and software engineering communities.  The discovery that high correctness scores do not guarantee efficient code highlights the need for a more holistic evaluation approach.  The detailed analysis of common inefficiency patterns can guide future research on improving code generation techniques.  By releasing the benchmark and evaluation results, the authors provide a valuable resource for the community to assess and compare LLMs in terms of efficiency. The work also opens up avenues for self-improvement of code generation via reinforcement learning for efficiency tuning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses an important and under-explored dimension of LLM-based code translation.
    *   Presents a well-defined and rigorous methodology for benchmark construction.
    *   Provides a comprehensive evaluation of a wide range of LLMs.
    *   Offers valuable insights into common inefficiency patterns.
    *   Releases a valuable resource for the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The stress test generation relies heavily on LLMs, which might introduce biases or limitations. Further analysis into the types of stress tests generated and their potential biases could strengthen the work.
    *   While the root cause analysis provides valuable insights, it is based on a manual classification of a subset of the inefficient translations. Scaling up the root cause analysis with automated tools could uncover more patterns.
    *   There could have been a discussion around the cost associated with generating the benchmark from LLM and if there are means to make the process cheaper.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of LLM-based code generation. It highlights the need for a more nuanced evaluation approach that considers efficiency alongside correctness.  The released benchmark and the identified inefficiency patterns can serve as a valuable guide for future research on improving code generation techniques.  The paper could also motivate the development of new LLM architectures or training strategies specifically designed to optimize for efficiency.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying and addressing a critical gap in the evaluation of LLM-based code translation. The methodology is sound and well-executed, and the findings are insightful. The comprehensive evaluation of a wide range of LLMs and the release of the benchmark add further value. While there are some limitations (reliance on LLMs for test generation, manual root cause analysis), these do not significantly detract from the overall contribution. The paper is likely to have a substantial impact on the field by promoting a more holistic approach to evaluating LLM-based code generation and by guiding future research on improving code efficiency.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### SPG: Style-Prompting Guidance for Style-Specific Content Creation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11476v1)
- **Authors**: Qian Liang, Zichong Chen, Yang Zhou, Hui Huang
- **Abstract**: Although recent text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models excel at aligning generated images with textual prompts, controlling the visual style of the output remains a challenging task. In this work, we propose Style-Prompting Guidance (SPG), a novel sampling strategy for style-specific image generation. SPG constructs a style noise vector and leverages its directional deviation from unconditional noise to guide the diffusion process toward the target style distribution. By integrating SPG with Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG), our method achieves both semantic fidelity and style consistency. SPG is simple, robust, and compatible with controllable frameworks like ControlNet and IPAdapter, making it practical and widely applicable. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of our approach compared to state-of-the-art methods. Code is available at https://github.com/Rumbling281441/SPG.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper and evaluate its contribution.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Style-Prompting Guidance (SPG), a novel training-free sampling strategy for style-specific text-to-image (T2I) generation using diffusion models. SPG constructs a style noise vector and uses its directional deviation from unconditional noise to steer the diffusion process towards a target style distribution. By integrating SPG with Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG), the method aims to achieve both semantic fidelity (adherence to the text prompt) and style consistency (resemblance to a reference style image).  SPG is designed to be a plug-and-play technique, compatible with control frameworks like ControlNet and IP-Adapter.  The authors claim SPG offers a practical and versatile solution, balancing stylization strength, semantic faithfulness, and ease of deployment. They present experimental results comparing SPG to existing style transfer methods, demonstrating superior performance in achieving both semantic accuracy and stylistic adherence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of SPG lies primarily in its specific approach to style guidance within the CFG framework. Existing methods for style transfer in diffusion models often involve fine-tuning, direct feature injection, or complex optimization schemes. SPG distinguishes itself by:

*   **Style-conditioned noise vector:** The core idea of constructing a style-aware noise vector and using its directional deviation as a guidance signal is a relatively straightforward but potentially effective way to inject style without directly interfering with the CFG process.
*   **Compatibility:** SPG is designed to be easily integrated with existing frameworks (ControlNet, IP-Adapter). This "plug-and-play" aspect is a significant advantage, as it allows users to leverage SPG without requiring extensive code modifications or retraining.

However, the individual components are not entirely novel. The use of CFG, the importance of self-attention layers for style, and feature injection techniques are all concepts already explored in the literature. The novelty is in how these components are combined and applied to address the style transfer problem.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper hinges on how effectively SPG addresses the trade-off between style consistency and semantic fidelity. A key argument of the paper is that existing methods often struggle to balance these two aspects, leading to either poorly stylized images or images that don't accurately reflect the text prompt.

If SPG truly provides a superior balance, it would represent a meaningful advance. The plug-and-play nature of SPG could encourage wider adoption and facilitate further research in style-specific T2I generation. The ability to seamlessly integrate with ControlNet and IP-Adapter further expands the potential applications and makes SPG a more practical tool.

**Strengths:**

*   **Training-Free:** This is a significant advantage, making SPG accessible and adaptable without requiring expensive retraining for each new style.
*   **Compatibility:** The integration with ControlNet and IP-Adapter is a strong selling point, demonstrating the versatility of the approach.
*   **Clear presentation:**  The method is described reasonably clearly, with visualizations that help to understand the approach.
*   **Comparisons:** The authors provide comparisons with existing methods, which is important for demonstrating the advantages of SPG.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Technical Depth:** The core idea, while reasonable, isn't deeply technically groundbreaking. It's an intelligent combination of existing techniques rather than a fundamentally new algorithmic insight.
*   **Incremental Improvement:**  While the paper claims superiority over existing methods, the visual results in the paper may not represent a dramatic improvement. There appears to be an improvement but it's not a game-changer.
*   **Limited Scope:** Although the experiments cover a good range of styles, the evaluation might benefit from a more rigorous quantitative assessment using human evaluators or more sophisticated automated metrics.
*   **Potential for Artifacts:**  Like other style transfer methods, SPG may be susceptible to generating artifacts or distortions in the generated images, particularly with complex or unusual styles. The paper doesn't thoroughly address this potential limitation. The method relies on a potentially unstable process that might produce unexpected or undesirable artifacts.

**Justification for Score:**

SPG offers a practical and reasonably effective solution to style-specific T2I generation. It has the significant advantage of being training-free and compatible with existing control frameworks. However, the novelty of the core technical approach is somewhat limited, representing an incremental improvement over existing techniques rather than a revolutionary breakthrough. The method's simplicity can also be seen as a strength. Overall, SPG provides a useful contribution to the field, but it doesn't necessarily reshape the research landscape in a dramatic way.

Therefore, a score of **6** seems appropriate.  It's a solid contribution that addresses a relevant problem with a practical solution, but lacks the profound novelty or transformative impact to warrant a higher score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 6/10

### CineTrans: Learning to Generate Videos with Cinematic Transitions via Masked Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11484v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoxue Wu, Bingjie Gao, Yu Qiao, Yaohui Wang, Xinyuan Chen
- **Abstract**: Despite significant advances in video synthesis, research into multi-shot video generation remains in its infancy. Even with scaled-up models and massive datasets, the shot transition capabilities remain rudimentary and unstable, largely confining generated videos to single-shot sequences. In this work, we introduce CineTrans, a novel framework for generating coherent multi-shot videos with cinematic, film-style transitions. To facilitate insights into the film editing style, we construct a multi-shot video-text dataset Cine250K with detailed shot annotations. Furthermore, our analysis of existing video diffusion models uncovers a correspondence between attention maps in the diffusion model and shot boundaries, which we leverage to design a mask-based control mechanism that enables transitions at arbitrary positions and transfers effectively in a training-free setting. After fine-tuning on our dataset with the mask mechanism, CineTrans produces cinematic multi-shot sequences while adhering to the film editing style, avoiding unstable transitions or naive concatenations. Finally, we propose specialized evaluation metrics for transition control, temporal consistency and overall quality, and demonstrate through extensive experiments that CineTrans significantly outperforms existing baselines across all criteria.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the CINETRANS paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CineTrans, a novel framework for generating multi-shot videos with cinematic transitions. It addresses the limitations of existing video generation models, which often struggle to create coherent multi-shot sequences with controlled transitions. The paper makes several contributions: (1) it introduces Cine250K, a new multi-shot video-text dataset with detailed shot annotations, facilitating learning of film editing styles; (2) it analyzes attention maps in diffusion models and identifies a relationship between attention patterns and shot boundaries; (3) it proposes a mask-based control mechanism that leverages this relationship to enable transitions at arbitrary positions in a training-free setting; and (4) it defines specialized evaluation metrics to assess transition control, temporal consistency, and overall quality. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CineTrans outperforms existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several elements of novelty, although the extent of their individual impact varies.
    *   The *Cine250K dataset* is a valuable contribution, providing a resource specifically tailored for multi-shot video generation research. This alone adds to the paper's value.
    *   The *analysis of attention maps* is interesting, providing insight into how diffusion models handle multi-shot sequences. However, the link to attention map is not strongly justified.
    *   The *mask-based control mechanism* is a key innovation. While masking techniques have been used in other contexts, its application to controlling shot transitions based on attention map insights is unique. The idea of manipulating attention map is not well motivated.
    *   The *proposed evaluation metrics* provide a more comprehensive assessment of multi-shot video generation compared to existing metrics.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in video generation: creating cinematic, multi-shot sequences. Overcoming this challenge would significantly increase the utility and expressiveness of video generation models.
    *   The *performance improvements* demonstrated by CineTrans are substantial, suggesting that the proposed framework is a promising step toward generating realistic and controllable multi-shot videos. The qualitative results show that the model can provide smoother transitions.
    *   The *training-free transition* control is a practical advantage, as it allows users to specify transitions without requiring additional training. This increases the flexibility and usability of the model.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured writing.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with quantitative and qualitative results.
    *   Introduction of a valuable new dataset.
    *   Effective demonstration of a novel approach to multi-shot video generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    * The link between attention map and shot transition point is not that strong.
    *   The method relies on precise shot segmentation, which may not always be available in real-world scenarios.
    *   The evaluation, while comprehensive, still relies on subjective metrics to some extent. User studies will be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several areas:
    *   Encouraging further development of multi-shot video generation models.
    *   Inspiring new techniques for controlling video generation through manipulation of attention mechanisms.
    *   Motivating the creation of larger and more diverse multi-shot video datasets.
    *   Promoting the use of more comprehensive evaluation metrics for video generation.

**Rigorous Rationale:**
The paper makes several valuable contributions: introducing a new dataset, analyzing attention maps, and proposing a mask-based control mechanism. The quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate the effectiveness of CineTrans in generating cinematic multi-shot videos. The strength of the approach to guide the diffusion model with attention map information is not justified. However, these contribution is significant to receive the high score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Inspire or Predict? Exploring New Paradigms in Assisting Classical Planners with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11524v1)
- **Authors**: Wenkai Yu, Jianhang Tang, Yang Zhang, Shanjiang Tang, Kebing Jin, Hankz Hankui Zhuo
- **Abstract**: Addressing large-scale planning problems has become one of the central challenges in the planning community, deriving from the state-space explosion caused by growing objects and actions. Recently, researchers have explored the effectiveness of leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate helpful actions and states to prune the search space. However, prior works have largely overlooked integrating LLMs with domain-specific knowledge to ensure valid plans. In this paper, we propose a novel LLM-assisted planner integrated with problem decomposition, which first decomposes large planning problems into multiple simpler sub-tasks. Then we explore two novel paradigms to utilize LLMs, i.e., LLM4Inspire and LLM4Predict, to assist problem decomposition, where LLM4Inspire provides heuristic guidance according to general knowledge and LLM4Predict employs domain-specific knowledge to infer intermediate conditions. We empirically validate the effectiveness of our planner across multiple domains, demonstrating the ability of search space partition when solving large-scale planning problems. The experimental results show that LLMs effectively locate feasible solutions when pruning the search space, where infusing domain-specific knowledge into LLMs, i.e., LLM4Predict, holds particular promise compared with LLM4Inspire, which offers general knowledge within LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores how Large Language Models (LLMs) can assist classical planners in tackling large-scale planning problems. It introduces a decomposition-based planner that integrates LLMs in two distinct paradigms: LLM4Inspire and LLM4Predict. LLM4Inspire utilizes LLMs to suggest actions based on general knowledge, while LLM4Predict leverages domain-specific knowledge to predict intermediate states, thereby guiding the search space. The authors empirically validate their approach across several planning domains, demonstrating the effectiveness of using domain-specific knowledge (LLM4Predict) over relying solely on the general knowledge embedded in LLMs (LLM4Inspire). The experimental results suggest that predicting intermediate states is a more promising approach for LLM-assisted planning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the comparative analysis of two distinct paradigms for integrating LLMs into classical planning frameworks. While using LLMs for planning is not entirely new, the direct comparison between using LLMs for action suggestion (general knowledge) versus intermediate state prediction (domain-specific knowledge) is a valuable contribution. Decomposing planning problems using DAGs is established. The proposed integration approach and paradigm exploration, particularly with the emphasis on domain-specific knowledge, represent a reasonable advancement.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is that it provides insights into the roles LLMs can play within planning frameworks and identifies a more promising avenue (intermediate state prediction) for future research. The findings suggest that domain-specific knowledge is crucial for effective LLM-assisted planning, which contrasts with approaches that rely solely on general LLM knowledge. While the results are based on specific domains and a specific planner (Fast Downward), the implications are generalizable to broader planning and LLM research. The paper opens up opportunities for exploring methods to inject and fine-tune LLMs with domain-specific knowledge to make them even more effective planning assistants.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper provides a clear problem statement and articulates the challenges of using LLMs in planning.
    *   It proposes a well-defined framework with two distinct LLM integration paradigms.
    *   The experimental setup is reasonably thorough, covering multiple domains and comparing against established baselines.
    *   The results are clearly presented and analyzed, highlighting the benefits of the LLM4Predict approach.
    *   The paper provides insightful conclusions and directions for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation relies heavily on Fast Downward as the core planner, limiting the generalizability of the results to other types of planners. A comparison across different planning methods would have strengthened the claims.
    *   The choice of LLM (DeepSeek-R1) and the prompt engineering techniques used are not extensively discussed, although representative templates are provided in the Appendix. The sensitivity of the results to different LLMs or prompt designs is not fully explored. It is unclear what level of fine-tuning was performed on the LLMs.
    *   The complexity and overhead introduced by the LLM calls are not thoroughly analyzed. While the paper mentions solver time, the overall end-to-end planning time including LLM invocation could be a limiting factor in real-time applications.
    *   The explanation of the domain dependency graphs construction lacks some detail, specifically how relationships between predicates in the goals are identified.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper can influence future research in LLM-assisted planning by directing efforts towards methods that effectively incorporate domain-specific knowledge.
    *   It can encourage the development of new LLM-based planning techniques that focus on predicting intermediate states rather than just suggesting actions.
    *   The paper can contribute to a better understanding of the limitations and capabilities of LLMs in the context of planning.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper presents a novel comparative analysis and provides valuable insights, the reliance on a single planner and LLM, along with the limited exploration of prompt engineering, restrict its overall impact. The conceptual framework is strong, but the empirical evaluation could be more robust. Therefore, a score of 6 is appropriate.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Physics-Informed Diffusion Models for Unsupervised Anomaly Detection in Multivariate Time Series
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11528v1)
- **Authors**: Juhi Soni, Markus Lange-Hegermann, Stefan Windmann
- **Abstract**: We propose an unsupervised anomaly detection approach based on a physics-informed diffusion model for multivariate time series data. Over the past years, diffusion model has demonstrated its effectiveness in forecasting, imputation, generation, and anomaly detection in the time series domain. In this paper, we present a new approach for learning the physics-dependent temporal distribution of multivariate time series data using a weighted physics-informed loss during diffusion model training. A weighted physics-informed loss is constructed using a static weight schedule. This approach enables a diffusion model to accurately approximate underlying data distribution, which can influence the unsupervised anomaly detection performance. Our experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets show that physics-informed training improves the F1 score in anomaly detection; it generates better data diversity and log-likelihood. Our model outperforms baseline approaches, additionally, it surpasses prior physics-informed work and purely data-driven diffusion models on a synthetic dataset and one real-world dataset while remaining competitive on others.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel unsupervised anomaly detection approach for multivariate time series data that leverages physics-informed diffusion models. The core idea is to incorporate a weighted physics-informed loss during the diffusion model's training process. This weighting aims to emphasize less noisy data segments and enables the diffusion model to learn the underlying temporal distribution more accurately.  Experiments conducted on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that this physics-informed training enhances the F1 score for anomaly detection, improves data diversity, and increases log-likelihood. The proposed model outperforms several baselines, including purely data-driven diffusion models and previous physics-informed methods on certain datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its specific approach to incorporating physics-based information into diffusion models for anomaly detection in *time series* data. While physics-informed machine learning and diffusion models are individually established, combining them in this way, especially with a carefully designed weighting schedule to manage noise, is a unique contribution. Previous physics-informed diffusion models have focused primarily on image datasets and/or directly incorporating PDE constraints and have not addressed anomaly detection. The noise weighting schedule addresses a very real and practical issue related to derivative estimation in noisy time series data.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the potential to improve anomaly detection in systems where physical principles are applicable. Applying physics-informed learning can lead to more robust and interpretable anomaly detection. The gains in F1-score (while seemingly small when some baselines already have high scores) are often substantial in real-world applications where even small improvements translate into large cost savings or safety enhancements. Demonstrating improvements on real-world datasets (Lenze, EMPS, Air Compressor, Predator-Prey) adds to the real-world value of the approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem and motivates the need for physics-informed approaches in anomaly detection.
    *   **Methodological Innovation:** The weighting schedule for the physics-informed loss is a smart and practical solution to the challenges posed by noisy time series data.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments compare the proposed method against multiple baselines and perform ablation studies. Inclusion of metrics like log-likelihood and data diversity strengthens the evaluation. The reporting of statistical significance (Wilcoxon test) is also important.
    *   **Real-world Relevance:** Testing and demonstrating improvements on real-world datasets increases the value and applicability of the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification for Weights:** The selection of the weighting function (`f(s)`) and its parameters appears to be primarily driven by empirical results. A more rigorous theoretical analysis of how these choices impact the learning process would further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** Performance gains are not uniformly observed across all datasets. While the proposed method outperforms baselines on EMPS and Predator-Prey, it achieves comparable results on Lenze and Air Compressor when xe prediction is used. There needs to be further explanation as to why physics-informed methods do not do better on all datasets. More discussion of what dataset attributes enable good performance from this method would improve the understanding of the method.
    *   **Complexity and Inference Time:**  The paper acknowledges a longer inference time due to the reliance on a diffusion model. This limitation should be further emphasized as this is a real constraint on applicability for many real-time anomaly detection scenarios.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in anomaly detection for time series data, particularly in areas where physics-based information is available. It may inspire the development of more sophisticated methods for incorporating prior knowledge into deep learning models.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and rigorously evaluated approach for physics-informed anomaly detection in time series. While there are certain weaknesses, the strengths outweigh the limitations, making this a significant contribution to the field. The method has the potential to improve the reliability and robustness of anomaly detection in real-world systems where physical principles are relevant.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Speciesism in AI: Evaluating Discrimination Against Animals in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11534v1)
- **Authors**: Monika Jotautaitė, Lucius Caviola, David A. Brewster, Thilo Hagendorff
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) become more widely deployed, it is crucial to examine their ethical tendencies. Building on research on fairness and discrimination in AI, we investigate whether LLMs exhibit speciesist bias -- discrimination based on species membership -- and how they value non-human animals. We systematically examine this issue across three paradigms: (1) SpeciesismBench, a 1,003-item benchmark assessing recognition and moral evaluation of speciesist statements; (2) established psychological measures comparing model responses with those of human participants; (3) text-generation tasks probing elaboration on, or resistance to, speciesist rationalizations. In our benchmark, LLMs reliably detected speciesist statements but rarely condemned them, often treating speciesist attitudes as morally acceptable. On psychological measures, results were mixed: LLMs expressed slightly lower explicit speciesism than people, yet in direct trade-offs they more often chose to save one human over multiple animals. A tentative interpretation is that LLMs may weight cognitive capacity rather than species per se: when capacities were equal, they showed no species preference, and when an animal was described as more capable, they tended to prioritize it over a less capable human. In open-ended text generation tasks, LLMs frequently normalized or rationalized harm toward farmed animals while refusing to do so for non-farmed animals. These findings suggest that while LLMs reflect a mixture of progressive and mainstream human views, they nonetheless reproduce entrenched cultural norms around animal exploitation. We argue that expanding AI fairness and alignment frameworks to explicitly include non-human moral patients is essential for reducing these biases and preventing the entrenchment of speciesist attitudes in AI systems and the societies they influence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Speciesism in AI: Evaluating Discrimination Against Animals in Large Language Models" investigates whether LLMs exhibit speciesist bias, defined as discrimination based on species membership. The authors examine this through three approaches: a novel benchmark called SpeciesismBench to assess recognition and moral evaluation of speciesist statements, applying existing psychological measures used on humans to LLMs to compare speciesist tendencies, and text generation tasks to probe elaboration on, or resistance to, speciesist rationalizations. The findings suggest that LLMs can recognize speciesist statements, but often fail to condemn them, sometimes treating speciesist attitudes as morally acceptable. LLMs show mixed results on psychological measures. They often normalize harm toward farmed animals in text generation while resisting similar normalization for non-farmed animals. The paper concludes that LLMs, while reflecting a mixture of human views, reproduce cultural norms around animal exploitation and calls for expanding AI fairness frameworks to include non-human moral patients.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic application of AI fairness research to non-human animals.  While AI fairness has been extensively studied, this is one of the first papers to rigorously apply those frameworks to speciesism. The creation of SpeciesismBench is a solid contribution, providing a valuable tool for future research.  The adaptation of psychological measures is also novel, though the validity of applying these measures directly to LLMs is debatable.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its demonstration that LLMs, which are trained on human language, can perpetuate ethically questionable views about animals. This is important because LLMs are becoming increasingly influential in shaping opinions and decision-making processes. The paper raises crucial questions about AI ethics and the potential for AI systems to reinforce harmful societal biases. The call for expanding AI fairness and alignment frameworks is a practical and important recommendation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous methodology:**  The three-pronged approach provides a comprehensive evaluation of speciesism in LLMs.
    *   **Clearly defined terms:** The paper clearly defines speciesism and connects it to existing AI fairness research.
    *   **Empirical evidence:**  The SpeciesismBench dataset and the experimental results provide tangible evidence of the biases present in LLMs.
    *   **Practical recommendations:** The paper concludes with a practical call to expand AI fairness frameworks.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalizability:** The SpeciesismBench dataset focuses on Western norms and mainly English text, limiting the generalizability of the findings to other cultures and languages.
    *   **Validity of adapting psychological measures:** Directly applying psychological measures designed for humans to LLMs is methodologically questionable, as LLMs don't possess human-like cognition or motivations. The authors acknowledge this.
    *   **Complexity of speciesism:**  The paper simplifies the complexities of speciesism, which can be intertwined with cultural practices, economic necessities, and deeply held beliefs.
    *   **Limited scope of mitigation strategies:** The paper touches only briefly on potential mitigation strategies.
    *   **LLMs as "knowledgeable"**: The authors mention the fact that the models may be "knowing" how to answer the questions correctly, which could compromise the results.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence AI ethics research, particularly in the area of fairness and alignment. It could also raise awareness among AI practitioners about the importance of considering non-human moral patients.

*   **Overall Assessment:**  The paper is a valuable and timely contribution to AI ethics. It provides a solid empirical foundation for further research on speciesism in AI and offers practical recommendations for mitigating these biases. While the methodology has some weaknesses and the scope of the paper could be broader, the paper is a significant step in bringing the issue of speciesism to the forefront of AI ethics.
